The number simply counted something other than what the caption claimed. **This is the fifth "renders a plausible number" finding in four days** (visitor-tick's pad, the dead `metrics/daily` path, the judge panel's two dark seats, the learning loop that never ran, now this), and the pattern holds: every one was found by a human happening to look.
  - **Measured before anything was changed.** `online24` 745, `online30` **ZERO**, `online5` **ZERO** — 745 people in a day and nobody at all in the last half hour is not a traffic shape, it is a signature. 518 of the 600 sampled sessions sat in a single 11 km cell in Dallas, still beating.
  - **The cell was not a bot in any useful sense. It was our own SEO surface working.** The daily rollup gave it up: 660 of the day's 747 sessions entered on `/today/{YYYY-MM-DD}`, across **576 DISTINCT dates**, 509 of them seen exactly once. `/today` is server-rendered, sitemapped, bounded at +/- 5 years, and its own netlify.toml comment says it exists so "Google crawls /today/2026-05-14 once and the content never changes". A JS-executing renderer walking ~3650 stable URLs is that design succeeding. **The crawl is WANTED. Counting it as people was the bug**, and it reached both the landing globe and /admin.
  - **That reframing is the whole entry, and it inverted the fix.** The first instinct was to identify and block a bot, which would have meant reading IPs (the endpoint deliberately stores none), sniffing user-agents (spoofable), or leaning on `lib/rate-limit.mjs` — which is still backed by per-isolate memory because `UPSTASH_*` was never set, so it would have looked like a limiter and been a lottery, exactly the 2026-07-28 finding. None of that was needed once the traffic turned out to be legitimate.
  - **Two fixes instead. (1) Per-cell cap on the read.** Each ~11 km cell contributes at most `CELL_CAP = 25` sessions to its pin AND to the counts, and a capped cell logs loudly, because it is either a datacenter or a milestone and both deserve to be seen. The `.count()` aggregate was DELETED rather than kept: it returns one number for the whole window with no way to see which cell it came from, so it could not be filtered and it was the number carrying the crawl. Its replacement is a floor bounded by `MAX_DOCS`, which is the safe direction for a figure printed on the landing page. Replayed live: 745 → 107, one cell capped, all 57 pins still on the globe.
  - **(2) The beat now requires a human.** `track.js` fired the first presence beat on load, unconditionally, which is what made this a counter of page renders. The first beat of a session now waits for one real interaction OR 20s of **visible** dwell (hidden time does not accumulate, so a prerendered or backgrounded tab never qualifies). `navigator.webdriver` vetoes outright, and the comment says why that is not sufficient on its own: automation frameworks set it, search renderers deliberately do not, so the dwell test is what covers them. Later beats are ungated, because `_da_plast` shares `_da_sid`'s sessionStorage lifecycle, so someone who engaged on page one is counted from page two without re-earning it.
  - **Verified in a browser, four cases, self-recorded at fixed checkpoints** — external timing could not prove an 8-second silence through tool latency: idle produced 0 beats at 3s/8s/15s with dwell firing at 20.9s; a scroll at 5s produced exactly 1 beat 0ms after the signal with the dwell timer torn down so it could not double-count; `webdriver` produced 0 beats through 25s; a warm tab beat at 3ms with `first:false`. Then live on the real page: `/today/2024-03-15` untouched gave 0 beats through 14s, and the same page with a scroll gave 1 beat 1ms later. **One early run read as a failure and was the harness, not the code:** the Browser pane reports `document.hidden`, which correctly suppressed every path. Worth remembering before trusting a negative result from that surface.
  - **The read bill, which predates all of this and was the larger number.** Cost is (cache misses per day) x (docs in the window), and at a 60s TTL with the crawl inflating the window to ~750 docs that was roughly **860K Firestore reads/day for an ambient globe** — the same drain shape the 2026-05-18 credit audit cut, on a project that has already had a day of site-wide 429s from a blown read cap. TTL is now 5 minutes and the gate takes the window to ~100 real sessions: about **29K/day**. A 24h pin set does not move minute to minute, and `online5`/`online30` riding the same cache are up to 5 minutes stale, which nothing sells liveness off.
  - **DISCONTINUITY, deliberate and stamped in three places.** `presence_daily.sessions` steps DOWN roughly 8x from 2026-08-14: before it a session was any page render, crawlers included; after it a session is a visitor who interacted or dwelled. The drop is the correction. New `engagedGate:true` on the daily doc records WHICH RULE produced a day's number, and the /admin card states it in prose rather than leaving an unexplained cliff in the sparkline. **Do not compare a window that spans it**, and do not read an unstamped day as if it meant the same thing. Same posture as the 2026-08-10 device-keying step-down.
  - **Accepted cost, stated so nobody "fixes" it later:** a real visitor who lands, touches nothing and leaves inside 20 seconds is not counted. That is an undercount, which is the honest direction for a number we might quote. Presence should mean presence.
  - **The durable lesson, and it is not the 2026-08-10 one.** That entry said a metric rendering a plausible number is not evidence it works. This one is narrower and worse: **both of these metrics DID work.** Every pin was a real request, every session a real render. The failure was entirely in the LABEL — "people", "active today" — asserting things the pipeline never measured. A number can be correct and its caption still be a false claim, and the caption is what a stranger reads.

- **One sign-in chooser, on every surface that has one** (2026-08-12). Email/password shipped 2026-07-25 into the SHARED modal, and the decision-log entry listed the five surfaces routed into it. Everything else kept its own hand-rolled `signInWithPopup(new GoogleAuthProvider())`, so on ~30 pages the only way in was still Google, and the emailed link landed into the same gap the day it shipped. Every user-facing sign-in control now calls `window.openAuthModal()` first and keeps its Google popup as the fallback for a page that loads without the script.
  - **The guard is worthless without the script, which is the part that nearly shipped broken.** `topbar.js` pulls `auth-modal.js` in site-wide, so the guard was assumed to work everywhere; it does not, because seven pages have no topbar (`/atlas`, `/floor`, `/ladder`, `/live-round`, `/tokens`, `/voice-rfd`, `/watch`). On those the new code silently fell through to Google and the sweep was a no-op. Caught by pressing the button in a browser rather than by reading the diff, which is the only way this class of thing gets caught: the code was correct and the dependency was absent. Those pages now carry their own `<script defer src="/js/auth-modal.js">`, the same pattern `read-aloud.js` already uses for non-topbar pages.
  - **Deliberately NOT swept, and each for a reason.** The four `/admin*` gates stay Google-only: they gate one person, so a wider chooser adds surface without removing a barrier. `tournaments.html`'s `withUser()`, `challenges.html` and `rounds.html` consume the popup's resolved user to continue an action mid-flow, and the shared modal finishes by navigating to `destination()`, which would drop the action the person was in the middle of. Routing those needs the flow to survive a reload first; that is its own change, not a line in a sweep.

- **The resolution takes both debaters to change, and the audience gets to ask for one** (2026-08-12, Aidan). Two coupled changes to `/live-round`, and the first is a correctness fix wearing a feature's clothes. **Either debater could silently rewrite the motion mid-round.** The in-round editor published straight to the round doc, and the peer's snapshot handler adopted `d.motion` on arrival, so one side typing in the resolution field, or pressing the random-motion button, changed what the other person was arguing with no notice and no way to object. A motion is the one thing both sides prepared against; changing it is a change to the contest, not an edit.
  - **An edit is now a proposal.** It writes `motionProposal` plus the proposer's own entry in `motionProposalAccepts`, and the visible resolution does not move until the opponent accepts. Proposing implies your own agreement, so nobody presses accept on their own idea. Declining and withdrawing are deliberately the same operation, clearing the proposal: either debater is entitled to end one, and nobody can be held to an open request.
  - **The Firestore rule is the part that matters**, and it is the same shape as `leaderboardConsentChangeIsOwnUid`: a writer may only change their OWN key in the accepts map. Clearing the whole map is allowed on purpose, since that is a decline, a withdrawal, or the commit after both agreed, and it can only ever remove agreement rather than manufacture it. Verified against the deployed ruleset from a real participant's browser: adding only the opponent's acceptance is denied, adding both at once is denied, adding your own succeeds. **One caveat worth writing down because it made a first test read as a hole: re-writing a key to the value it already holds is not a change, so `diff().affectedKeys()` does not report it and the write is correctly allowed.** Test consent guards from a CLEAN map or you will measure nothing.
  - **A round with one identified debater still edits directly.** Direct-link entry and an opponent who has not arrived have nobody to ask, and consent needs a second party to be consent.
  - **The audience can now say what it wants argued.** New Topics tab in the audience deck: suggest a motion, vote on other people's, ranked by votes so the room's preference is what a debater sees between speeches. A seated debater pre-Speech-1 gets "Put it to the round" on any suggestion, which drops it into the same consent flow. **The crowd sets the agenda; it does not seize the round.** A suggestion is a request that still needs both debaters, which is the same boundary the audience judge notes have.
  - **Named accounts only, matching `audienceEvals` rather than comments.** Anonymous auth is free and unlimited to mint (the 2026-07-28 rate-limit lesson), and a suggestion here can become the actual resolution, so a guest lane would let one person fill the board with throwaways and manufacture a consensus that is not there. Comments and group chats stay guest-open; their blast radius is the chat UI. Votes are an array of uids with a rule allowing you to add or remove only your own, and the `votes` integer that drives the ordering query is pinned to `voters.size()` so the ranking cannot be forged by a single writer. All four rejections verified live against the deployed rules.

- **Every link out of a live round was a trapdoor, not just the two that were guarded** (2026-08-12). The site shell in `js/live-pip.js` has kept a call alive while its owner browses since 2026-06-26, and the feature worked. It was simply wired to **two** anchors, `.bar-logo` and `.bar-back`, so the brand wordmark and "Back to board" rode along while every topbar tab, Explore item, profile link and footer link on the same page hard-navigated and tore the Daily iframe down mid-round. Someone checking the leaderboard between speeches dropped their opponent. The intercept now takes any same-origin link that would actually change the page, and deliberately does NOT take: a modified or middle click, `target="_blank"`, `download`, off-site and `mailto:`/`tel:` destinations, or a hash on the current page (in-page movement, not navigation). Those are all someone asking for a new window or staying put, and hijacking them would be the opposite bug. **Same shape as the ready-check finding a day earlier: the feature was real, its coverage was two selectors wide, and nothing said so.** Paired with it, the shell bar's "Return to the round" went from a 12px pill the same size as "Leave round" to a 44px target with ~2.6x the area, because getting back in is the only reason that bar exists and the destructive button should not be competing with it for the eye.

- **The emailed sign-in link comes back, and it leads for anyone creating an account** (2026-08-12, Aidan: "allow ppl to make accounts with facebook or other stuff beyond google yk allow less barriers of entry"). **This REVERSES the 2026-04 removal and the 2026-07-25 "magic links stay retired" line.** Both were written under a rule that no longer applies: the 2026-04 reasoning was "two paths = confusion" when the alternative to Google was nothing at all, and by 2026-07-25 the site already had two email paths anyway. The barrier being removed is real and specific: a password is a thing you have to invent at the door and remember on the way back, and neither is work a debater came here to do.
  - **Passwordless was OFF on the live Firebase project and is now ON.** `signIn.email.passwordRequired` flipped to false via the Identity Toolkit admin API (`PATCH /admin/v2/projects/debateos-78ac5/config`), which enables the email-link provider while leaving password sign-in fully intact. Verified by actually sending one from a preview build rather than by reading the config back: an unconfigured provider answers `auth/operation-not-allowed`, and this returned a real send.
  - **Which email path leads depends on who is standing there.** Creating an account defaults to the link, because that person has no password and asking them to make one is the barrier. Signing in defaults to the password form, because someone who set one will reach for it. The exception is a device whose last successful sign-in WAS a link, which now records as its own `emaillink` family in `debateos-last-signin-method` rather than collapsing into `email`; without that distinction a link-only user comes back to a password field they never filled in and has no way to know the link is still an option. Either path is one tap from the other, and a typed address carries across the switch.
  - **The return trip is handled on load, everywhere, without opening anything.** `completeEmailLink()` runs when auth-modal.js loads (topbar.js pulls it onto ~85 pages), gated on a cheap `oobCode` + `mode=signIn` URL test so a normal page never pays for the Firebase SDK to find out it has nothing to do. It links the anonymous account the same way the Google path does, since `js/notifications.js` signs nearly every visitor in anonymously and `currentUser` is therefore almost always a truthy anonymous user, and falls back to a plain sign-in on `credential-already-in-use` rather than failing the trip. The continue URL is wherever they were, so the link does not cost them their place. Spent link parameters are stripped with `replaceState` so a refresh does not re-run a used code and show an error on a page that just worked.
  - **A link opened on a different device still asks for the address**, which is Firebase's requirement and also the thing that stops a forwarded or leaked link from signing in whoever found it.
  - **NOT built, and it needs Aidan, not code: Facebook and Microsoft.** Both are one config call away on our side (the same admin API that enabled passwordless takes `defaultSupportedIdpConfigs/facebook.com` with a client id and secret), but the id and secret come from a Facebook app at developers.facebook.com and an Azure app registration, and creating those accounts is not something an agent should do on someone's behalf. Apple is a third case worth knowing: it is ALREADY enabled on the project, but its `clientId` is the iOS bundle id, so the web chooser still gates it behind `window.__DB_NATIVE`. Web Apple sign-in needs a separate Services ID plus key from the Apple Developer account before that gate can come off.

- **A streamed ballot now heartbeats through the model's thinking pause, and /judge stops blaming the user for our dropped connection** (2026-08-12). A user wrote in: `/judge` returned "The ballot came back in a shape we could not read. Try again, or trim the transcript if it is very long" on every attempt. They changed the transcript, changed the instruction prompt, cut it to five lines with two speakers, tried Edge signed in and Naver Whale signed out. Every time. **The error was ours and the message sent them to fix their own input.**
  - **Measured three ways rather than guessed.** Through `/api/claude` the request died at **13.6s having last received bytes at 3.0s**: 639 bytes, `message_start` plus a `content_block_start` opening a **thinking** block, no `message_stop`, HTTP **200**. Straight to `api.anthropic.com` the identical request **completed at 22.4s with `message_stop`**, and Anthropic sent its own `ping` at 9.0s to hold the connection open across a 6.5s silent gap. The model was never the problem. A reasoning model opens a thinking block and goes quiet, and the edge drops a stream that stops producing bytes.
  - **The fix is a heartbeat in the passthrough**, so it covers every streaming consumer of `/api/claude` rather than one page: a `: keepalive` SSE comment when nothing has arrived for four seconds. **Only at an event boundary**, which is the part that matters: chunk boundaries can split an SSE event mid-JSON and a comment spliced into one would corrupt the exact payload this is protecting. `X-Accel-Buffering: no` rides along. Verified after deploy on the same failing request: the two 13-byte keepalives land at 8.3s and 12.3s, Anthropic's own ping follows at 14.6s, and the stream runs to completion at 42.1s / 52 chunks. The Korean transcript that failed 100% of the time now returns a full ballot with speaker points.
  - **Checked every other streaming consumer before trusting it.** `exhibition` and `high-school` skip non-`data:` lines outright; `index` (two parsers) leaves the line intact and `JSON.parse` throws inside a `try`; `practice` and `learn` gate on `startsWith('data: ')`. A comment line is inert in all five. Non-streamed requests bypass the wrapper entirely, so JSON responses are untouched.
  - **`/judge` also stops mislabelling transport as content.** An empty extraction is now `transient` and retried three times with jittered backoff, the same treatment live-round ballots got on 2026-07-28, and the copy no longer tells someone with five lines to trim their transcript.
  - **The lesson, and it is the same one as the truncating judge panel:** a reasoning model's silence is not an error, but every layer between it and the browser treats silence as a dead connection. **When a model can go quiet for ten seconds, something has to keep the pipe warm, and the failure will present as your parser's fault.** A 200 with 639 bytes and no terminating event is a transport failure wearing a content failure's clothes; label it as one, or a user will spend an afternoon rewriting input that was fine.

- **The AI listens in and fact checks to the audience** (2026-08-12). A live round has an AI judge listening and an audience watching, and when a debater states a flatly false number the room has no way to know. New `/api/fact-check` reads the speech as it is delivered and raises a card to everyone watching when a claim is checkably, badly wrong. **It is not a judge and the ballot never reads it.** It never scores, never says who is winning, and it lives in its own band under the transcript rather than over the video, because a fact check that competes with the round for attention is a fact check nobody reads by the second speech.
  - **One client checks, everyone reads.** The driver is a participant whose bench is NOT speaking: idle, already holding the live transcript through the round snapshot, and picked as the lowest uid on that bench so a 2v2 does not fire four identical checks. Flags go on the round doc, which is publicly readable, so an anonymous spectator sees the same card the debaters do and the broadcast view renders it as a lower-third.
  - **The speaker is the one person who does not see it live.** A card naming their bad number, delivered to them mid-speech, is coaching, and coaching from the house in a judged round is worse than an uncorrected statistic. Their own client holds cards from their current speech until they hit End.
  - **The quote gate, same discipline as the clash map.** Every flag carries a verbatim quote and it is verified against the transcript; a flag whose quote is not there is dropped. Broadcasting "they said X, and X is false" when nobody said X is the one failure this surface cannot absorb.
  - **It takes two passes, and the reason is measured, not theoretical.** Run against a deliberately ACCURATE speech, a single pass returned two cards at 0.93 and 0.98 confidence, one of which moved a correct 1991 date to 1997 and called the speaker false. A checker that invents a correction is worse than no checker, because the audience cannot tell which of the two is wrong and the house is the one holding the microphone. So every candidate goes to a second call whose job is to REFUTE it, defaulting to refusal, and only survivors are published. Verified over four runs each way afterwards: a fabricated Supreme Court case was carded 4/4, and the accurate speech published nothing 4/4 (it produced two candidates, both refused).
  - **A nit gate in code, because the prompt could not hold the line.** The checker twice wanted to card "wage theft in 84% of restaurants" and correct it to "violations in 83.8%" at 0.97 confidence: right figure, standing point, useless card. A `distorted` flag whose correction restates the same figures is now dropped in code. It reads spelled-out numbers, since the quote side is a transcript of someone SAYING a figure; it compares years exactly, since 2019 and 2022 are three years apart rather than 0.1%; it ignores a stray date the speaker never gave, which is how one "in its 2021 investigations" smuggled the nit back through; and it stands aside for existence denials, since "no source supports a forty percent collapse" has to repeat the figure in order to deny it.
  - **Perplexity `sonar-pro` is the checker** so a card arrives with sources the audience can open; with no key it falls back to Claude at a higher confidence bar and the card says "model knowledge, no live sources" rather than implying it was checked. Ceiling of 10 passes a round, one per 20s and only on 55+ new words, so a full round is bounded. `scripts/test-fact-check.mjs` (59 assertions) runs in the pre-commit hook, and every guard was shown to fire by mutating the code it covers.

- **Pricing contradiction closed: $550/season Program wins** (2026-08-11, Aidan's call). soul.md and the doctrine had been pointing at two different answers for a school roster ($550/season in doctrine §13 vs a $20/yr, later $50/yr, Team tier on the pricing page), and agents were frozen on pricing surfaces until Aidan picked. He picked $550. Team is not deleted, it is demoted: $50/year for a study group, explicitly not a program license. Code side, /pricing already carried both a Team card and a Program card, so the only live copy that contradicted the decision was `app/schools.html` selling the Team tier's "1,500 requests a month across 50 seats" to coaches on the schools page. Fixed to the season license. §7 rewritten to the live 7-tier lineup (it had drifted: it was missing Voice $12/mo, Tokens $4.99/mo, and Program entirely). Standing rule for agents from here: never quote a school a seat plan.

- **The automatic waiver runs to entries close, not to Friday Aug 22. This is the written reason the entry below demands** (2026-08-19, Aidan's call: "we just need a hundred + people taking part"). The entry model is UNCHANGED: $20 for prize eligibility, free entry plays the identical field, the fee waived for anyone who asks. `entryFeeCents` stays 2000, both doors stay in the markup, comps still count separately on `compedEntries`. The only thing that moved is `FOUNDING_CUTOFF_MS`, from `2026-08-22T23:59:59-04:00` to `2026-08-29T23:59:59-04:00`.

  **Why, given the entry below deliberately made it a date.** That entry gave two reasons for a cutoff over a blanket: a blanket "would delete the paid door", and a comp for existing accounts only would be an unforwardable loyalty gift. The second reason is the one that has now inverted. The cutoff was set on Aug 11 to make ONE email forwardable. The job on Aug 19 is a ten-day bracket-fill push into a field of 7 against a target of 100, and against that job the same cutoff is the obstacle: it expires on day 3, so for seven of the ten days every stranger the push reaches meets a $20 door whose only bypass is emailing the organizer to ask. No stranger sends that email. The cutoff stopped making the outreach worth sending and started making it impossible.

  **What it costs: nothing, measured.** Eight days after entry opened, `paidEntries` was **0** against `entryCount` 7. The $20 door has never taken a payment, so no revenue is being given up, only a field. The pot is funded by the organizer and never raised from fees (the published rules say so in as many words), so widening the waiver cannot shrink a prize.

  **Why it is not the fifth flip the log forbids.** The rules already promise the fee "is waived for anyone else who asks, on identical terms." The waiver was universal on request the whole time; what changed is that it no longer costs an email round-trip. The paid door survives in markup and in `entryFeeCents`, so a late entrant to a FUTURE tournament meets it again with a fresh cutoff. Nothing here reads a data value to decide whether a promise is on screen, which is the architecture rule from the entry below.

  **Surfaces moved together**, per that entry's own list: `lib/founding-comp.mjs` (the constant plus `FOUNDING_CUTOFF_LABEL`, which is why the page, the email and the rules cannot drift), the doors and hydration copy and cost FAQ in `tournaments.html` including JSON-LD `validThrough`, sections 3 through 6 of `tournament-rules.html`, the `admin-open-reminder.mjs` header, and `js/topbar.js`, whose site-wide strip had its own separate `FREE_ENDS` constant still counting down to Friday on every page. **NOT moved: the poster PNGs**, flat images that still say $20. Tolerable on the same logic as before, understated rather than wrong, since $20 remains the real list price that the waiver removes. **Open for Aidan: the landing FAQ and the rules still describe the waiver as something you ask for. That is true and safe, but during the push the stronger and equally true line is that entry is free right now.**

- **The waiver becomes automatic for everyone who signed up early, and the announcement email goes out** (2026-08-11, Aidan's call, same day as the entry decision below). The $20 prize entry STAYS and this is not a fifth flip of the entry model. It is the waiver that decision already promises ("waived for anyone who asks, on identical terms"), granted automatically to a bounded cohort instead of one email at a time: **any account created on or before 11:59 PM ET on Friday, August 22, 2026 enters the prize bracket free.** Two reasons it is a date and not a blanket. A comp for everyone would delete the paid door, which is the flip the log forbids. A comp for existing accounts only would be a loyalty gift with no acquisition edge, and the whole point of mailing the list is that a recipient can forward it and the person they forward it to can still qualify. So the cutoff is what makes the email worth sending.
  - **Qualification reads Firebase Auth account creation, never `user_profiles.createdAt`.** Sign-in writes to Auth and nothing mirrors the timestamp into the profile doc, so a profiles-based check would have silently disqualified most of the list. Same trap `scheduled-spar-night` hit with emails on 2026-07-22, and worth stating as a habit: **the profile doc is where preferences live, Auth is where the account facts live.**
  - **A comp writes the SAME `tournaments/{tid}/payments/{uid}` record a Stripe payment writes** (`status:'comp'`), so every eligibility read keeps working and there is one source of truth about who plays for the cash. It does NOT touch `prizePoolCents` or `paidEntries`, because a comp contributes no money and a pot that counts it would be lying; comps land on `compedEntries` instead. It does NOT skip the 18+ attestation, because a comped entry is prize-eligible and cash is 18+. And it does NOT resurrect the self-serve `/api/entry-waiver` retired hours earlier: a plain free entry is still cash-ineligible, and the only way to a $0 eligibility record is the server checking a date it read from Auth.
  - **New field `prizeEligible` on the entry, and the distinction is deliberate.** `paidEntry` means money moved and stays true only for Stripe; `prizeEligible` answers "can cash reach this entry" and is true for both doors. Collapsing them would have made a comp read as revenue. `entry-checkout` now refuses a comped user with `ALREADY_ELIGIBLE` before Stripe is reached, because the one bug in here that costs a real person real money is charging someone who owes nothing.
  - **Surfaces moved together**, per the rule in the entry below: the doors in `tournaments.html` (the paid door now leads with Free and keeps a fallback pay button for anyone who does not qualify), its hydration copy and cost FAQ, sections 3 through 6 of `tournament-rules.html`, and the landing `#tournament-band` fact row. **NOT moved: the poster PNGs, which are flat images that still say $20.** That is tolerable in a way it would not be for a flip, because $20 remains the real price for a late account; it is understated, not wrong.
  - **The email is a button, not a cron.** `/api/admin/open-announce` previews the cohort by default and sends only on an explicit confirm, in batches, stamping `openAnnounceSentAt` per user so a stopped run resumes and a double-click cannot double-send. It refuses outright if no public tournament is in registration, because the email's whole promise is a live entry page. It also **refuses to send from an unverified sender domain** rather than discovering the problem in an error tally: only debateai.com is verified on Resend, `lib/email.mjs` still defaults to a gmail From for the older senders, and that exact mismatch is why Spar Night sent zero emails between 2026-07-22 and 08-05.
  - **Known cohort limit, surfaced rather than fixed: accounts with no `user_profiles` doc are not mailed at all**, because `isOptedOut` treats a missing profile as opted out and mailing an address whose preferences we cannot read is the wrong side to err on. The preview counts them separately so the size of that hold-back is visible before deciding whether it is worth its own pass.

- **Entry is $20 with a waiver, and the tournament runs all day as drop-in. This SUPERSEDES the free-entry entries below** (2026-08-11, Aidan's call, confirmed twice in the same session). The entry model flipped four times in one day across parallel sessions: paid on 2026-08-10, then "Competing is free, permanently; advertising is the intended funding" (`522ee0a6`), then paid-with-a-waiver (`a75314ec`), then free again with the surfaces swept to match (`a8d5995d` and the log entry above it). Each flip was defensible on its own and each one left a surface behind, which is how the site ended up selling a $20 door that the endpoint refused. **The decision of record is now: $20 for prize eligibility, free entry plays the identical field, and the fee is waived for anyone who asks, on identical terms.** The waiver is what makes the fee defensible rather than exclusionary, so it is not a footnote: it renders on the paid door itself, not only in the rules. **Do not flip this again without an entry in this log that says why**, and if it does flip, the surfaces that must move together are: the two doors in `tournaments.html`, the eligibility and cost sections of `tournament-rules.html`, the landing `#tournament-band` facts, the poster PNGs (flat images, so their copy cannot follow the page), and `entryFeeCents` on the tournament doc.
  - **Promotion leads with the money; drop-in is a logistic, not a pitch** (2026-08-11, Aidan, same day). The entry model above is UNCHANGED. What changed is the landing band's headline, which read "Drop in any time. $850 in prizes." and now reads "Win $500 for winning an argument." Aidan's steer was blunt: do not advertise the drop-in aspect, advertise winning money. Convenience only sells a thing the reader has already decided to want, and a stranger scrolling past has not. The timing facts stay exactly where someone who HAS decided will look for them: the date line, the fact row, and `/tournaments`, whose "Turn up whenever. Debate. Leave." run-of-day section is deliberately untouched because it answers how the Saturday works rather than pitching it. `/tournaments`' own hero was already money-forward ("Get paid to win an argument") and needed nothing.
  - **Architecture note that outlives the price.** The 2026-08-10 failure was a door hidden by a JS branch reading `entryFeeCents`, so stale data resurrected a payment promise. The lesson taken from it then was "delete the paid door", and the durable version is narrower: **never let a data value decide whether a promise is on screen.** Both doors now render unconditionally in markup and the amount is stated in copy; the doc is set to match. If they ever disagree, checkout is the authority and the page is wrong, which is one line rather than an invisible branch.
  - **Format: all day, drop in whenever, no fixed start** (Aidan, same session). **SUPERSEDED 2026-08-25: the event runs in ONE session at 10:00 AM ET, strictly. See the entry at the top of this log for why the liquidity argument below inverted.** There is no check-in window and no round-one call. Doors open 10:00 AM ET, entrants arrive when it suits them, get paired, play as many rounds as they like, and standings come from the rounds they actually played. Entries close in the evening, the top of the board goes to single elimination, and the final streams. This is the /spar liquidity fix applied to an event: a fixed start time on a small base means everyone has to be free at the same hour, and an all-day pool only needs two people awake at once. **Open mechanical question, flagged not solved: `lib/tournament.mjs` pairs globally per ROUND over a ranked field, which assumes a synchronous round structure.** Drop-in play needs either continuous pairing (the spar matchmaker, with results counting toward the board) or rounds that are really time-windows. The copy now promises drop-in; the engine has not been reconciled with it, and that is the next real build, not a copy edit.

- **Three of the six advertised brains were dead, and now something checks** (2026-08-12). Found incidentally while auditing the judge panel, which is the whole problem: **Gemini returned 429 with its prepayment credits exhausted, the xAI key was invalid, and `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` had never been set on Netlify.** So `/pricing`, the landing, and the `/judge` picker all sold six brains while three of them errored on click, and the only reason anyone found out was unrelated work on a different subsystem. Claude, GPT and DeepSeek were fine. **This is the fourth "renders a plausible screen while broken" finding in two days** (the judge panel, its `degraded` flag, the learning loop's missing indexes, now this), and the pattern is the point: every one was found by a human happening to look, which is not a strategy.
  - **New `/api/brain-health` probes every brain with a tiny completion against the SAME model the picker offers**, because a working key against a retired model id is still a dead brain to a user. Public and keyless on purpose, same posture as `/api/judge/charter`: `/judge` reads it to grey out a dead brain rather than letting someone pick one that fails after they paste a whole transcript, and that read has to work signed-out. **The provider's raw error is not public** (redacted to a reason category: auth / billing / rate_limit / model / timeout / unconfigured / down) because "your prepayment credits are depleted" names an account's billing state; the raw text is added for an authenticated admin, who is the person who can act on it.
  - **Classification reads the body before the status, and that ordering is load-bearing.** Providers overload codes: xAI returns 400 for a bad key and Google returns 429 for exhausted prepaid credit rather than for rate. Status-first would have sent whoever is on call to the wrong console, so the classifier is tested against the real observed responses rather than against the HTTP spec.
  - **No cron, deliberately.** It probes lazily when a request finds the 15-minute shared cache cold, which costs cents a month, and only an admin can force a re-probe. A visitor able to spend provider calls with a query parameter is a drain vector, and an interval job doing this would be the exact shape the 2026-05-18 credit audit killed.
  - **Surfaced where it will actually be seen:** an "AI brains · live probe" card on `/admin` with a per-brain fix line ("the account is out of credit, top it up") rather than a bare "down", plus an attention-strip entry so a dead brain competes for attention with the other health signals instead of waiting to be scrolled to. 19 assertions in the pre-commit hook, five mutations applied one at a time (leak the provider message, read status before body, send `max_tokens` to gpt-5, probe a brain the picker does not offer, mutate the caller's payload during redaction) and every one caught. The roster is asserted to match `js/judge-options.js` in BOTH directions, because that drift is the failure this whole endpoint exists to catch.
  - **Gemini turned out to be broken twice over, and only the probe made that visible.** The endpoint reported `model`, not `billing`, which was surprising enough to check: `gemini-2.5-pro-preview-05-06`, the id pinned in the `/judge` picker and in `gemini.mjs`'s allow-list, now 404s as not-found, and so do the other two ids that allow-list carried. So even with billing restored, every Gemini call would have failed on a dead model id. **A preview id is a rental, not a pin**, and nothing was re-checking. All three swapped for ids verified against the account's own model list, and the retired ones are deliberately NOT kept as fallbacks, because a silent fallback to a dead model is exactly the failure this had. The corrected action list is therefore longer than the first read suggested: Gemini needs BOTH a billing top-up and the id refresh (id done here), and Open Lab's `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` turns out to exist in the production-only context and be REJECTED rather than missing, so it needs regenerating rather than adding.
  - **ACTION, and it is not code:** top up Gemini billing, regenerate the xAI key, regenerate the OpenRouter key. Roughly five minutes each in three consoles. Until then the picker greys them out honestly, but the site still advertises six brains and serves three.

- **The judge panel was two-thirds dead, and the fix is a new season: current models, a fenced persuasion axis, and every ballot naming what decided it** (2026-08-12). The 2026-07-30 charter promised a three-family panel and the code delivered one; what nobody had done since was RUN it. Measured against a real three-speech round on 2026-08-11, with production keys: **the Anthropic seat returned no ballot at all** (`claude-sonnet-5` called at `max_tokens: 900`, spent all 900 on reasoning, hit `stop_reason: max_tokens` mid-JSON, failed to parse, recorded as a missing vote), and **the Google seat returned HTTP 429, prepayment credits depleted**. So the panel had at most one live juror, below the quorum of two, which routes to the single-judge fallback, which calls the same truncating config. **Nothing about this was visible as an error.** The audit row said "juror failed" and the charter said "degraded", both correctly, and nobody was reading either. It is the same shape as the visitor counter and the learning loop: a subsystem rendering a plausible number for months. **The durable lesson is narrower than "test your code": reasoning models bill thinking against `max_tokens`, so a cap tuned before they existed turns into a parse bug the moment a model is upgraded under it, and it presents as your parser's fault.** `BALLOT_MAX_TOKENS` is now 3000 (widest measured ballot: 2064) and lives in one constant.
  - **The panel is re-pinned to each family's current top tier, and every id was run against the real ballot prompt before it was pinned:** `claude-fable-5` at effort low (16.1s), `gpt-5.5` at reasoning-effort low (13.5s), `gemini-3.6-flash` (dark, see below). End-to-end through the real shared runner, both surfaces: valid ballots, five scored axes, panel wall 16 to 24 seconds. **Effort is pinned and audited alongside the model id**, because a ballot is a bounded task against a published method and the top reasoning tiers spend minutes re-deriving it: `low` returned clean ballots on every seat and roughly halved wall clock (32.4s to 16.1s on Anthropic). An undisclosed effort would be exactly the quiet dial this charter exists to remove. `gpt-5.x` also rejects `max_tokens` outright and needs `max_completion_tokens`, which is why the juror dispatch now branches on model family and why adding an id to a brain proxy's allow-list is not sufficient on its own.
  - **The Google seat ships dark and says so.** The account's Gemini billing is exhausted, so that juror returns a 429 in under half a second and the panel runs two-handed. Pinning it anyway is the honest option: two agreeing jurors still carry a verdict, a two-handed split records unresolved (which is correct, not a workaround), and restoring the seat is a billing top-up rather than a code change. DeepSeek is wired as a standby family, persona and all, so re-pinning the third seat is genuinely one line. **ACTION: top up Gemini billing to get the third juror back.** Same outage kills `/api/gemini` for every other surface.
  - **Persuasion is scored, and the fence is the feature.** New rubric `adjudication-2026-08b` adds a fifth scorecard axis. A method that scores only the flow quietly teaches debaters that being understood does not count, which is a strange thing for a product about speaking. So persuasion scores whether the comparative actually landed on a reasonable listener hearing it once: concrete stakes, a world you can picture, an argument built to be understood the first time. It explicitly **never** scores charm, confidence, volume, pace, fluency, polish, vocabulary, accent, or dialect, it sits **last** in the weighing order, and it can only break a tie the flow left genuinely level. Without that fence it is a delivery score wearing a better name, and it would fall hardest on second-language debaters, so the fence is asserted by the test suite rather than trusted to a prompt.
  - **Every ballot names the issue that decided it**, and on a panel the jurors' *reasons* are clustered and published next to their votes. Three judges agreeing on the winner for three different reasons is a weaker result than the vote count suggests, and reporting only the count flatters it. The clustering is deliberately crude (stopworded token overlap, no model in the loop) because a semantic judgement made by another model is the exact circularity this layer exists to avoid; it under-reports agreement, which is the safe direction to be wrong in for a number we might quote. Live proof from the end-to-end run: two jurors, same winner, `agreement: 1.0`, `issueAgreement: 0.5`.
  - **Paradigms: two new lenses, and every lens now states what it costs you.** New `Did you move me` (the persuasion lens, same fence) and `Teaching chair` (identical call and identical bar, with the ballot spent on what to fix; a lens that went easy on the newer debater would be unfair to their opponent, so the prompt says so explicitly). Every card gained a **Costs you** row, which is the fairness change: a paradigm system is only fair if the downside of agreeing to one is knowable in advance, and a card listing only what a judge rewards is an advert. Two new out-of-bounds rules back it: **no penalty for a norm nobody stated**, and **an agreed paradigm may sharpen a burden both sides accepted but may never invent one**, with ambiguity read the way that costs nobody the round.
  - **One defect I shipped and caught by opening the page.** `/judge`'s paradigm `<select>` is hardcoded markup rather than built from `js/judge-options.js`, so adding the two lenses to the data file left the dropdown without them and every deep link from the guide page landed on an empty selection. Fixed, and the class is now guarded: the suite parses the paradigm keys out of the data file and asserts both that the dropdown offers every one and that no guide card deep-links to a paradigm the picker cannot select. Worth stating as a habit rather than a bugfix: a data file and a hand-written control that are supposed to agree will drift, and reading the diff will not tell you, because both sides look right on their own.
  - **Guarded, and the guards were shown to fire.** 194 assertions, up from 145, covering the persuasion fence, the weighing position, the panel pin's completeness, effort validity, dispatch and persona coverage per pinned family, and issue-consensus maths. Per the standing habit, the suite passing on the first run proves nothing: sixteen deliberate mutations were applied one at a time (strip the delivery disclaimer, promote persuasion up the ladder, delete the fence, pin a provider with no dispatch entry, pin a bogus effort, collapse the panel to one family, report a lone juror as agreeing with itself, count stopwords as agreement, drop the deciding-issue rule, break contiguity) and every one was caught. One mutation initially "passed" and that was the harness's fault, not the guard's: the anchor string existed in both rubric versions and the edit landed on the retired one.
  - **A third silent gap, found by running the finished code rather than reading it: `degraded` was measured in configured keys, not in votes.** The flag was `available.length < wanted.length`, which only catches a juror whose API key is unset. A juror whose provider returns 429 or times out counted as available, cast no vote, and the ballot still reported `degraded: false`. So the dark Google seat produced full-strength-looking ballots, and `/api/judge/reliability` was undercounting degraded panels using exactly the rounds that were degraded. Now `tally.votesCast < wanted.length`. This one matters more than it reads: silent degradation is the specific thing the 2026-07-30 charter forbids, and the layer had been failing its own promise in the one direction nobody would notice.
  - **And the same mistake, one layer down, which broke every commit in the repo for a few hours.** The renderers were fixed to take the four original axes as a floor; the PARSERS were left strictly all-or-nothing. So a four-axis ballot produced no scorecard at all where it used to produce four, and `scripts/test-judge-run.mjs` (a guard a parallel session had just added around the shared runner) started failing on `origin/main`, which blocks the pre-commit hook for everyone. Fixed by giving `parseDims` and the panel's `medianDims` the same floor the renderers already had: a REQUIRED axis missing drops the card, an OPTIONAL one missing does not. **The lesson is not "run the other test", it is that a fix applied at one layer of a pipeline is not applied.** Half the change was correct and the incorrect half was the half nobody had a reason to look at. Guarded now: the suite asserts a five-axis ballot keeps five, a four-axis ballot still renders four, and a missing required axis still drops the card.
  - **Renderers were the quiet risk.** Both scorecard renderers validated dimensions all-or-nothing across a fixed axis list, which was right while the list was fixed and becomes a bug the instant it grows: adding an axis under that rule would have silently deleted the scorecard from every previously judged round. They now require the four original axes as a floor and render anything beyond them.
  - **`/judge` runs `claude-opus-5`, not Fable, and that is a drain decision rather than a quality one.** The page is public and anonymous; roughly double the per-ballot price buys a difference no debater would notice on a single transcript. The recorded rounds that actually move standing and settle credits are judged by the pinned panel, where the Anthropic seat *is* `claude-fable-5`. Switching `/judge` is one word in `js/judge-options.js` if that trade ever flips.

- **The AI judge stops being a single point of failure: published rubric, three-family panel, human appeal, fee neutrality, permanent audit** (2026-07-30). The judge was the one component where our own thesis conceded a hole: AI-judge resolution is circular. That was survivable while it was only an epistemic problem. It stops being survivable the moment credits, standing, or money settle off a verdict, because "an AI judge the operator owns, tunes, and can silently update" is house control of the outcome, which is the argument that sank Papaya and the argument a plaintiff's lawyer makes in the first paragraph. Five coupled changes, all state in Firestore.
  - **(1) The rubric is published before the round, versioned, and HASHED.** New `lib/judge-charter.mjs` is a pure module holding a season calendar and the plain-language criteria the adjudication core implements: the ten tests, the six-step weighing order, the four scorecard axes, the deadlock ladder, and the five things the judge may not do (no model repair, no invented arguments, no rewarding a flatly false unanswered claim, no deciding on what the judge would have argued, no debater instruction that names a winner). `rubricHash()` is a sha256 over a KEY-SORTED canonical serialization, so the digest follows content rather than object literal order: a reformat does not read as a rubric change and a real change cannot hide behind one. Every judgment now stamps `seasonId`, `rubricVersion`, and `rubricHash` **as of when it was judged**, not as of today, so a July ballot stays attributable to July's criteria forever. `RUBRIC_VERSION` is gone as a constant; that was the thing that made the rubric a moving target. The calendar is append-only and contiguous, with the pre-2026-07-30 era recorded honestly as `published:false` rather than retroactively reclassified under the new rubric.
  - **(2) The judge is a panel of three model families, not one call.** The season pins `claude-sonnet-5`, `gpt-4o`, and `gemini-2.0-flash` (the ids this repo's own brain proxies pin). Three calls to ONE model would measure sampling temperature and produce a flattering agreement number that means nothing, because the correlated error is the model itself; three families measure whether the round was decidable. Majority carries, medians (not means) set the points so one outlier juror cannot drag a score, and the load-bearing rule is that **an even split is NOT tie-broken**: any tie-break rule is a thumb on the scale and it would be our thumb. **Narrowed 2026-08-28:** no winner is reserved for a COMPLETE tied panel. A short panel, including 1-1 with one missing judge, stays pending and retries because provider failure is not a result. A true no-winner records `resolution:'unresolved'`, voids any market at face value, preserves both argument scores, and enters the ladder as a Glicko draw so both rating records update without manufacturing a winner. Panel calls run concurrently so wall clock is the slowest juror, not the sum, which keeps a three-family panel inside the sweep's existing budget. **Degradation is disclosed, never silent:** a missing provider key or a failed juror runs the panel short, and if quorum cannot be reached the ballot falls back to a single judge STAMPED `degraded`, surfaced by the public charter endpoint. `JUDGE_REQUIRE_PANEL=1` refuses instead of degrading; the default keeps rounds moving, because a missing key should not freeze every ballot on the site.
  - **(3) Inter-rater reliability is published, warts included.** `/api/judge/reliability` reports **Fleiss' kappa** on the binary winner rather than a raw agreement percentage, because raw agreement is inflated by any panel that leans one way: a judge that always said Prop would score 100% and measure nothing. Kappa nets that out. Published alongside it: the unanimity rate, the unresolved rate (a system that quietly resolved its split cases would show a better number and be worth less), the median juror spread in speaker points, and a count of any round where jurors did NOT receive identical prompts, which would make their agreement a measurement of the prompt. **Nothing is reported as a result under 30 rounds** (`reportable:false`); a kappa over four rounds is noise and dressing noise as documented reliability is the exact overclaim this system exists to avoid.
  - **(4) A human appeal layer, and no model may touch it.** Either debater can appeal within 72 hours on six stated grounds. Filing sets `disputeState:'open'`, which `verdictFrom` has always treated as a refusal and which nothing had ever set, so settlement genuinely freezes. A reviewer is disqualified by having debated the ROUND, not by role, so an admin who was in it cannot rule. Outcomes are upheld / overturned / void, a reason of 20+ characters is mandatory and public, and the original ballot is never deleted: it moves to `original` and the correction lives beside it, which is the only version that survives being subpoenaed. An overturn does not invent new speaker points, because a human reviewer flipping a call is not claiming to have re-scored four axes. **An appeal that cannot reach paid-out credits is decoration**, so this needed real reversal machinery: new `reverseMarket` in `lib/settle.mjs` writes negative compensating ledger entries namespaced by revision, un-settles positions so the corrected winner can be paid from the same intact pool, and backs out Sharp Score inputs; new `reverseRoundRating` in `lib/rating-apply.mjs` reverses the rating delta and the win/loss counts. Both state their limits on the record rather than faking a clean rewind: Glicko rd and volatility are path-dependent and are NOT reconstructed, and a streak is reset rather than guessed. Balances are allowed to go negative on a clawback, deliberately, because clamping at zero would make the ledger's arithmetic false and credits are non-redeemable so an underwater balance costs nothing real. `scripts/test-judge-integrity.mjs` asserts the appeal bench contains no provider call and no cron: routing an appeal back to a bigger model is the same circularity with a larger bill.
  - **(5) Fee neutrality made structural, and money gated on provenance.** Settlement is pari-mutuel with no rake term anywhere, so the operator earns the same whichever side wins. That is now enforced rather than promised: the test reads `credits.mjs` and `settle.mjs` and fails the build on any rake, fee, commission, vig, or house account. Verified adversarially by injecting a rake and confirming the guard fires. Coupled fix: **`MONEY_VERDICT_SOURCES` restricts settlement to `verdictSource:'server'`.** `judgment.mjs` has recorded provenance honestly since it was written and nothing acted on it; live-round ballots are written by a PARTICIPANT'S OWN BROWSER, and a verdict authored by one of the two interested parties settling an economy is worse than the house writing it. Stated consequence: **live human rounds do not settle credits until their ballot moves server-side.** The ladder and the public record are unaffected. No live impact today, since nothing creates `markets` documents yet, which made this the right moment to close it.
  - **Everything is logged, in the cloud, immutably.** New `judge_audit/{judgmentId}`: season, rubric version and hash, every juror's provider and model id, whether each model was the season pin or a **disclosed override**, every raw vote including the dissents, the panel outcome, a hash of the exact prompt each juror received, timings, and failures. Public read, server write, never updated in place; corrections append to a `revisions` subcollection, the same posture as the credit ledger. Full prompt text is deliberately NOT stored: it is ~60KB of identical adjudication core per juror per round, and the rubric hash plus the prompt hash plus the transcript already reproduce the input, so a copy on every row would be a large cost for no evidentiary gain.
  - **Surfaces:** public `/judge-integrity` renders the live charter and reliability figures from the two open endpoints (so the page cannot drift from what the judge is actually pinned to), linked from `/judge` and `/judge-paradigms` and in the sitemap. `/api/judge/charter` and `/api/judge/reliability` are open and keyless so anyone can fetch the charter, hash it, and confirm the criteria that judged a round. Round pages now publish panel agreement next to the verdict and render dissenting jurors with their reasoning intact rather than blended into the majority RFD. **Bug caught while wiring this:** `rounds.html` read the winner as `winner==='prop' ? prop : opp`, so a null winner would have announced the Opposition as victor, which is the precise opposite of what a split panel means; guarded, along with the sweep's ballot email.
  - **Guarded by a hook, not just a test.** `scripts/test-judge-integrity.mjs` (145 assertions) joins the price guard in `scripts/hooks/pre-commit` and hard-blocks a commit that breaks any of these promises, because each one is published to users and quotable in a dispute. Two of its guards were found VACUOUS by deliberately weakening the code they cover: the 1-1 split passes on quorum rather than on the majority rule, so the real 2-2 case (clears quorum, still must not resolve) was untested and is now covered. Worth repeating as a habit: a test suite that passes on the first run has not yet been shown to test anything.

- **The default format is now a spec'd lay format, judged out of 30** (2026-07-28). Quick Clash was the format nobody had written down: 3/3/2/2 with 2 minutes of prep, four rules of one sentence each, and a ballot scored on the APDA 23-30 speaker scale, which is a circuit instrument being pointed at people who have never seen a round. Aidan's one-page lay spec replaces it. **Structure:** constructive then rebuttal, one each a side. The sheet says 5/5/5/5 with 5 minutes prep; shipped as **5/5 constructives, 3/3 rebuttals, 5 minutes prep**, taking the sheet's own flagged risk ("maybe a shorter rebuttal?") rather than a 25-minute default. The four `rules` strings were rewritten to carry the shape: the second constructive does two jobs (answer their case, then build its own), rebuttals bring no new arguments, and every argument is claim then warrant then impact. **Scoring:** a 30-point lay ballot, 15 for the overall argument, 10 for responses to the opponent's case, 5 for structure and comprehension. Highest total wins, so **a weaker case can take the round on responses plus structure**, and the judge is told to open the RFD by saying so when that is what happened. `speakerPoints` stays on the 23-30 scale and stays out of the decision, because the leaderboard series is comparable across formats and a 22/30 rubric total posted into it would read as an incoherent round. The rubric rides a `rubric` key on the ballot JSON and renders as a scorecard under the verdict (and in the text export); if a brain omits it, the ballot renders exactly as before. **The plumbing this needed already existed and was dead:** `singleJudgeBallot` has carried a `{{formatJudgingCriteria}}` placeholder since it moved server-side, and no caller ever filled it, so the literal token was being sent to the model on every ballot. All three call sites in `practice.html` now pass `fmt.judging`, which makes per-format judging criteria a one-key-per-format edit for any future format. **Same rubric on the human surfaces:** live-round's `quick` (the "General" default) got the speech times and the 30-point JUDGE_RULES, and /judge's `quick` got the rubric in prose. **Cost note, deliberate:** AI speech length scales off `speech.time` at ~170 wpm, so the default format's AI turns grew roughly 65% in tokens. That is real drain on the format 56% of rounds use, and it is the trade for a format that is actually specified. **Left alone on purpose:** voice-debate's `quickclash` mode is a separate 2-minute voice drill with its own wiring, so "Quick Clash" now means two different lengths depending on surface; aligning it is its own pass. The /live narration script still says rounds take ten minutes for Quick Clash, and narration does not regenerate when page copy changes.
- **2v2 partner matching, a real tournament engine, and spectator admission** (2026-07-28). Three coupled builds that turn "we could host a tournament" into infrastructure. Closes the open item left by the 2026-07-27 tournament-readiness pass, which ended: *a real bracket/schedule data model once an actual tournament is scheduled (none exists yet)*. **(1) 2v2 partner matching.** A 2v2 needs a partner, an opposing team, and four people awake at once. `/partners` + `partner-match.mjs` own the first: a duo forms either by invite code or through an open `partner_pool` with a mutual-accept handshake, and it NEVER auto-completes the way a spar match does, because a partner is a bigger commitment than an opponent. `duo-pair.mjs` owns the second, and the load-bearing decision there is that the queue doc is keyed by **team, not user**: per-user docs would make a 2v2 a four-way atomic write where any half-commit strands a real person in a room their partner never entered, while team-keyed it is the same two-document transaction spar-pair already runs in production. Either partner can queue the team and the other does not need to be at their screen, because requiring four simultaneous humans instead of two plus two means it never fires at this traffic level. Every cross-user write is admin-SDK server-side, the lesson spar-pair was rebuilt on. **(2) live-round seats two per bench.** A 2v2 stays a two-BENCH round (format spec, transcript tags and ballot all reason about gov vs opp), so partner uids ride the existing benches rather than a new four-side model. Without them `mySide()` returns null for both partners and classifies them as SPECTATORS, locking them out of their own round. New `seatForSpeech()` gates control to one partner at a time; ungated, both hold live controls for every speech on their side and the round desyncs the first time they disagree about whose turn it is. Verified per format: APDA correctly gives seat 1 the PM and the PMR. **BP/Worlds in a four-person room is a COMPRESSION, not real 4-team BP, and is labelled as such.** **(3) tournament engine.** `lib/tournament.mjs` is pure and deterministic: seeded, so a draw can be reproduced when a coach asks why they hit the same opponent twice. It pairs **globally over the ranked field, not per win-bracket** — per-bracket pairing looks right and fails late, because two teams share a record precisely because they just debated, so small brackets fill with prior opponents and the only move left is the rematch (measured: 4 rematches in a 12-team field over 5 rounds, side skew reaching 3). Side pressure sits inside the matching plus a 2-opt repair, since choosing the partner is what keeps sides even; assigning sides to a fixed pairing can only pick the lesser of two evils. 1045 assertions in `scripts/test-tournament-pairing.mjs`. Odd fields are allowed side skew 2 on purpose: the bye breaks side parity and flattening it would cost an unearned pull-up, which is the worse trade. **(4) spectator admission.** Watching was open; anyone with a link dropped into the audience and debaters learned from the count ticking up. Now **public rounds require admission** (discoverable by strangers) while **unlisted rounds admit link-holders automatically** (the link is the invitation). Reuses the `joinRequests` surface with `kind:'watch'`, minus the 20s auto-decline, which would bounce nearly every spectator since debaters are mid-speech. Honest framing: an admission gate, not access control, because the round doc stays publicly readable for indexable completed rounds. **(5) partner quiz + match emails.** The pool is asynchronous in a way the spar queue is not: a spar match resolves in a minute while you watch a spinner, but finding a partner takes hours and nobody sits on a page that long, so without email the pool only fires when two people happen to be on the same screen at the same moment (the same liquidity problem Open Spar Night was built for). Emails go out on exactly two moments, proposal received and team formed, with addresses resolved server-side from Firebase Auth so the proposer never learns the other person's address, sent AFTER the transaction commits so nobody is mailed about a partnership that rolled back. New `partner` stream with its own `partnerOptOut`, deliberately not suppressed by the digest flag (an alert that a named person wants to team with you is closer to a reply than to a mailing list). A five-question quiz (level, goal, speaking position, availability, intensity) ranks the pool by fit with reasons shown, because an unranked pool's only signal is who refreshed most recently. Scoring rule: **complementary beats identical on speaking position, identical beats complementary on everything else.** Calibrating it caught two self-contradictions before ship, both worth remembering as a pattern: a pair who both wanted to speak first scored "Strong match" directly above the words "You both want to speak first," and a format mismatch was the biggest single deduction while never appearing in the reason line. **Costs held flat by design:** a round's pairings ride inline on the round doc so a draw is one read regardless of audience, and responses are shared-cached, per the 2026-07-28 load audit. **Deliberately NOT built:** results are entered by the director. The AI judge already writes a ballot, so auto-ingest is the obvious next step, but a tab that silently accepts machine results has no answer when one is contested; that wants a human confirmation step and a real tournament to calibrate against first.

- **Tournament load: voice metered per user, spectator counting made linear** (2026-07-28). A readiness audit ahead of running real tournaments found two things that break at venue scale, both invisible at today's traffic. **(1) The voice rate limit locked out whole buildings.** `realtime-session.mjs` ran `checkRateLimit('rt_' + ip)` at 6/hour and 10/day BEFORE the auth and plan gate, with no exemption for signed-in or paying callers. A school runs one NAT'd public IP, so the 7th voice round started in that building in an hour got a 429 no matter who started it or what they had paid: a paying user blocked by a stranger's practice round two tables over. Now the caller's token is verified first (JWKS-cached, no Firestore reads) and **named** signed-in callers meter per UID at 30/hour and 100/day, while anonymous callers stay per IP at the old 6/10. **"Named" is a same-day correction to this change, caught by probing the live endpoint after deploy.** The first version handed the per-UID lane to any valid token, and this app uses Firebase ANONYMOUS auth for guests (`isNamedAccount()` in firestore.rules gates on `sign_in_provider != 'anonymous'`). Anonymous accounts are free and unlimited to mint, so that replaced a hard 6/hour/IP ceiling with NO ceiling: rotate a throwaway anonymous account and the IP layers never apply again. Per-account damage was bounded at 2 by `FREE_VOICE_LIFETIME_LIMIT`, but the accounts themselves were not bounded, and `/api/realtime-session` mints real billable OpenAI Realtime sessions (confirmed by curling it unauthenticated: App Check is not enforcing because the site key was never filled in, so the IP layers are the ONLY gate on that endpoint). The lane now requires a named provider and fails closed onto the IP lane when the claim is missing. Tournament debaters sign in with Google or email, so the venue fix is unaffected. Live-verified after the fix: anonymous callers get exactly 6 before the 7th returns 429. Real entitlement was always enforced further down (`FREE_VOICE_LIFETIME_LIMIT`, plan lookup, token balance), so the per-UID layers only need to stop one runaway account and can be generous. The token is verified once and reused by the usage gate, not twice. **The limiter also only pretended to work:** it was a module-scope `Map`, so each Netlify isolate kept its own counter and a cold start wiped it. Under light traffic that reads as a working limit; under load it is a lottery. Moved to a new `lib/rate-limit.mjs` (Upstash-backed, in-memory fallback, reusing the env vars `claude.mjs` already documents). **ACTION: `UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_URL` and `UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_TOKEN` are NOT set on Netlify**, so the shared-counter half is dormant and both this and `claude.mjs` still fall back to per-isolate memory. The per-UID keying fix works regardless. Same pass fixed a spoofable key: the IP now prefers Netlify's `x-nf-client-connection-ip` over the client-supplied `x-forwarded-for`, whose whole raw string was previously the rate-limit key, so one caller could mint unlimited distinct keys by varying a header. **(2) Spectator counting was quadratic.** Every viewer on `/live-round` ran an unbounded `onSnapshot` over the `watchers` subcollection while every spectator wrote a heartbeat every 30s, so the reads behind the "N watching" pill grew as N SQUARED: roughly 5K reads/min at 50 spectators, 80K at 200, 500K at 500 (about $18/hour for a counter), and every browser in the room reprocessed all N documents on every beat, which is what actually made big rooms janky. The obvious fix is Firestore's `count()` aggregation, and it does not exist here: **aggregation is modular-SDK only, and `Query.count` is undefined on the compat builds this page loads. Verified in-browser against 10.5.0 and 11.10.0; do not "restore" it without re-checking that.** Instead the count folds into the presence beat that seated debaters already write to the round doc every 30s, and every client in the room already listens to that doc, so distributing the number is free. Cost is now about 4N reads/min total regardless of audience size (two debaters reading N watcher docs per beat) and a spectator pays nothing: about 2K reads/min at 500 spectators against the old 500K. The freshness filter runs server-side on `ts`, so stale heartbeats never cross the wire; `ts` is a plain field so the automatic single-field index covers it, verified with a live query (no composite index to deploy). Capped at 1000 with a "1000+" display so one enormous room cannot quietly become the expensive case again. Deliberate trade-off: with nobody seated (spectator-only replay viewing) the number holds its last value instead of dropping to zero, because a frozen count beats a wrong one. **(3) A ballot had exactly one chance to succeed.** `generateBallot()` in `live-round.html` was a single fetch with no retry, and its failure card offered a "Try again" button wired to `location.reload()`. A tournament ends many rounds inside the same minute, so that endpoint takes a burst of 8000-token Sonnet calls at the moment it matters most; `claude.mjs` passes the Anthropic response straight through, so one upstream 429 or 529 ended the round with no ballot and a button that reloaded the page back into the same overloaded window. Now bounded to 4 attempts with backoff at roughly 2s, 5s, and 12s, each JITTERED, because N clients retrying on an identical schedule is just a second thundering herd. An explicit `Retry-After` is honored (capped at 30s). Retryable: 408, 425, 429, 500, 502, 503, 504, 529, plus network failures and PARSE failures, since a truncated or malformed generation is exactly the transient case a retry fixes and the attempt budget bounds the token cost. Auth and plan errors (401/402/403/400) fail fast because they never self-heal. Worst case is about 20s of retrying before the failure card appears, and that card now retries in place instead of reloading, keeps the round view, and says plainly that the transcript is saved. New `live_round_ballot_failed` GA4 event carries status and attempt count so a real tournament leaves evidence. **Not a code fix but load-bearing operationally: anonymous callers share `claude.mjs`'s per-IP anon caps (5/min, 15/hour, 30/day) across a whole venue, and signed-in users bypass them entirely. Everyone signs in before an event, or the building splits 30 requests a day.** Audited alongside and found clean: the deployed Firestore ruleset was byte-identical to `origin/main` (checked against the Rules API, not assumed), coach emails are correctly segregated into admin-only `atlas_contacts`, `public_profiles` carries no contact fields by design, and Daily rooms cap at 8 participants but spectators never join the video, so that is a debater-plus-judge ceiling and not an audience limit.

- **Landing chapters keep content-visibility after the gate opens: landing_cv_v1 A/B** (2026-07-27). A renderer-memory pass found the landing's `content-visibility:auto` block (shipped 2026-05-27, extended since) covers all 13 below-fold sections but was being neutralized exactly when it matters: the 2026-07-25 "See more" gate shipped with a `content-visibility:visible !important` rule that force-renders every chapter the moment the gate opens, so an engaged visitor paid full layout+raster for ~7k lines at once. New `landing_cv_v1` experiment (sticky 50/50, `da-lpcv-ab`, `?lpcv=cv|control` QA force, registered on the shared `__abAssignments`/experiments.js rail, gate-toggle tagged `data-ab-test` and `landing_more_toggle` now carries the arm): the 'cv' arm scopes that override to `html:not([data-lpcv="cv"])` so chapters keep `auto` post-open and render only near the viewport; control keeps today's behavior byte-identical, as do JS-off visitors. Shipped alongside for both arms: per-section `contain-intrinsic-size` re-measured at 1280px and 375px (a `max-width:760px` override block; the old blanket 800px was off by ~1000px on #faq), and the 8 dead selectors from the 2026-05-27 list pruned (.price-compare, #founder-video, #overview, #debate-ai, .topics-edu-pair, .landing-guides-block, .landing-compare-block, #philosophy no longer exist in the document). Deep links through the gate got the founder-scroll treatment in the cv arm: `revealHashTarget` force-renders chapters above the target, lands, then re-lands at 450ms; #waitlist (outside the gate but after every chapter) joins the same path when the gate is open. Measured in isolated Chrome 150 (localhost, analytics blocked, 2 runs per arm + mobile): gate-open to next paint 43-54ms cv vs 124-161ms control; renderer-process RSS in the open-at-top state 267-278MB cv vs 338-353MB control (~70MB, ~20% lighter); anchors settle within 0.5px in cv on desktop and mobile (control parks ~41px past every chapter target, a pre-existing bias from the chapter strip relocating on open, left as-is for experiment purity); scrollspy walks all 13 sections; the #ranked-band sticky rail pins at exactly 72px; FAQ toggles work; identical console noise across arms. The #live-now globe canvas is outside the containment list, so its zero-width fallback sizing is untouched. Watch `ab_impression`/`ab_conversion` for `landing_cv_v1` plus `landing_more_toggle.cv` in GA4; if the cv arm wins, the follow-ups are deleting the scoped override rule and giving the gated `.hero-founder-frame > .hero` (founder scene, still uncontained) its own entry.

- **Round-corpus SEO unblocked from both ends: async rounds get indexable /r/ pages, the /practice publish CTA becomes an experiment** (2026-07-27). sitemap-rounds.xml was serving a valid but empty urlset: `public_rounds` has 0 docs (months of the passive publish card converted nobody) while the async relaunch's completed public rounds lived only at /rounds?r={id}, a client-rendered view whose single canonical points at /rounds, so Google never saw them. Two coupled fixes. (1) **r.mjs falls back to async_rounds**: completed public human-opened rounds render server-side at /r/{id} (turn transcripts with Prop/Opp labels and clocks, ballot + bold speaker points, crowd-vote strip, Article JSON-LD, per-round canonical) and join sitemap-rounds.xml through one shared gate, `indexableAsyncRound()` in lib/async-rounds.mjs. The gate excludes AI-seeded challenges twice over: the 'seed-' id prefix AND prop.uid=='ai', because pre-2026-07-22 sweep passes created AI challenges under plain auto-ids; live Firestore confirmed one such doc that a prefix check alone would have leaked to Google once answered. Hidden, unlisted, incomplete, and transcript-less docs are excluded too; excluded-but-existing ids 302 to /rounds?r={id} so shared links never dead-end. Honesty boundary per the Arena decision: AI inventory is never presented to Google as user rounds. Media stays on /rounds (turns hold Netlify Blobs references); the /r/ page renders transcripts and links "Listen to this round". rounds.html's share button now hands out /r/{id} for indexable rounds so inbound links stack on the canonical URL. At ship time zero async rounds qualify (the only completed round is an AI-opened seed), so this is plumbing that fills as human rounds complete. (2) **practice_publish_cta_v1 A/B on /practice**: 'card' arm keeps the old passive purple card below the fold; 'spotlight' arm renders the same publish action inside the ballot, directly under the verdict and points, with primary styling. Sticky per-browser assignment, ?publishCta=card|spotlight QA force (forced arms excluded from telemetry), impressions counted only when the CTA is 50% visible (the ballot mounts long after experiments.js boots, so a React ref mirrors sendImpression's event shape and sessionStorage dedupe key), clicks ride experiments.js data-ab-test, and round_published carries publish_cta_arm. Both arms signed-in only, since publishing requires auth; the anonymous sign-in card gains one sentence noting that signed-in debaters can publish. Results in Mission Control once each arm passes 20 impressions.

- **Keep the Arena separate from the landing page while it proves itself** (2026-07-27). `/arena` is a standalone live debate lobby, surfaced through the Explore menu without replacing `/`. It combines real public activity where available with clearly labeled seeded broadcast previews when live inventory is quiet. The honesty boundary matters: preview matches, markets, and schedules must never masquerade as current real events. Its visual-authenticity pass uses archived round photography, raw webcam-style stills, signed-in users' own profile photos, and lightweight charts. Generated portrait headshots were rejected because they made the community feel synthetic. Real leaderboard names never receive invented portraits.

- **The tournament front door becomes a spectator lobby** (2026-07-25). `/tournaments` now leads with one job: tell a visitor whether a featured round is live and what will happen when it starts. The first screen pairs a plain waiting-state explanation with a dark watch-room frame where the video, speakers, sides, and official motion will appear. Four selectable sample motions give people something concrete to explore while they wait, and a three-step Waiting, Live, Decision guide explains the full viewing flow.
